Stochastic Compositional Minimax Optimization with Provable Convergence Guarantees
Yuyang Deng, Fuli Qiao, Mehrdad Mahdavi

Abstract
Stochastic compositional minimax problems are prevalent in machine learning, yet there are only limited established on the convergence of this class of problems. In this paper, we propose a formal definition of the stochastic compositional minimax problem, which involves optimizing a minimax loss with a compositional structure either in primal , dual, or both primal and dual variables. We introduce a simple yet effective algorithm, stochastically Corrected stOchastic gradient Descent Ascent (CODA), which is a descent ascent type algorithm with compositional correction steps, and establish its convergence rate in aforementioned three settings. In the presence of the compositional structure in primal, the objective function typically becomes nonconvex in primal due to function composition. Thus, we consider the nonconvex-strongly-concave and nonconvex-concave settings and show that CODA can efficiently converge to a stationary point. In the case of composition on the dual, the objective function becomes nonconcave in the dual variable, and we demonstrate convergence in the strongly-convex-nonconcave and convex-nonconcave setting. In the case of composition on both variables, the primal and dual variables may lose convexity and concavity, respectively. Therefore, we anaylze the convergence in weakly-convex-weakly-concave setting. We also give a variance reduction version algorithm, CODA+, which achieves the best known rate on nonconvex-strongly-concave and nonconvex-concave compositional minimax problem. This work initiates the theoretical study of the stochastic compositional minimax problem on various settings and may inform modern machine learning scenarios such as domain adaptation or robust model-agnostic meta-learning.



1
Introduction

We consider the following minimax optimization problem:

\displaystyle\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}}\max_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{Y}}F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}):=h(\mathbf{x})+f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-r(\mathbf{y}),

(1)

where
f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is a stochastic compositional function. The compositional part can be defined over
\mathbf{x}
,
\mathbf{y}
or both
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{y}
, which correspond to the following three scenarios:

•

Composition on
\mathbf{x}
:
f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}):={\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta\sim\mathcal{S}_{f}}[f({\mathbb{E}}_{\xi\sim\mathcal{S}_{g}}[g(\mathbf{x};\xi)],\mathbf{y};\zeta)]

•

Composition on
\mathbf{y}
:

f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}):={\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta\sim\mathcal{S}_{f}}[f(\mathbf{x},{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi\sim\mathcal{S}_{g}}[g(\mathbf{y};\xi)];\zeta)]

•

Composition on
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{y}
:

f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}):={\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta\sim\mathcal{S}_{f}}[f({\mathbb{E}}_{\xi\sim S_{g}}[g(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y};\xi)];\zeta)]
,

where
\mathcal{S}_{f}
and
\mathcal{S}_{g}
capture inner and outer stochasticity in composition, respectively.

Many practical machine learning problems can be cast as an instance of the above optimization problem, such as robust meta learning
[
4
]
, AUC maximization
[
44
]
, statistical discrepancy minimization
[
26
]
, multiple source domain adaptation
[
14
,
25
,
5
]
, and stochastic compositional constrained programming
[
40
]
. Here we briefly describe several important applications in machine learning that motivate the proposed compositional minimax problem (
1
).

AUC Optimization
[
41
]

AUC optimization has been a powerful training paradigm that can mitigate the training data imbalance issue in classification problems.
Consider a binary classification problem with label
y\in\{-1,+1\}
, and model parameter
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Our goal is to solve the following problem:

\displaystyle\min_{\mathbf{w},a,b}\max_{\theta\in\Omega}

\displaystyle\Phi(\mathbf{w}-\alpha\nabla L(\mathbf{w}),a,b,\theta)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi(\mathbf{w}-\alpha\nabla L(\mathbf{w}),a,b,\theta;\mathbf{x}_{i},y_{i}),

where
L(\mathbf{w})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell(\mathbf{w};\mathbf{x}_{i},y_{i})
. Usually, the objective has a negative quadratic dependency on dual variable
\theta
, which renders this objective a nonconvex-strongly-concave minimax problem.

Robust MAML
[
4
]

In task robust MAML, our goal is to learn a model that can perform well over all distributions of the observed tasks, hence making it robust to task shifts in the testing time. The objective can be formulated as the following minimax problem:

\displaystyle\min_{\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\max_{\bm{\alpha}\in\Delta^{N}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha(i)L_{i}(\mathbf{w}-\eta\nabla L_{i}(\mathbf{w}))

where
\Delta^{N}
denotes simplex,
L_{i}(\mathbf{w}):=\frac{1}{n_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}\ell(\mathbf{w};\xi_{j})
,
N
is the number of tasks with task
i
having
n_{i}
samples. It is a nonconvex-concave compositional minimax problem, with composition on the primal variable.

Stochastic Minimization with Compositional Constraint
[
40
]

In many risk management problems, such as conditional value-at-risk (CVaR)
[
30
]
, risk-averse mean-deviation constraint
[
3
]
, or portfolio optimization with high-moment constraint
[
12
]
, we are tasked with solving the following programming with compositional constraint:

\displaystyle\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(\mathbf{x})\ \quad\text{s.t.}\ \quad

\displaystyle g_{1}({\mathbb{E}}[g_{2}(\mathbf{x};\xi)])\leq 0.

To solve the above constraint problem, one may consider solving the unconstrained variant:

\displaystyle\min_{\mathbf{x}}\max_{\lambda}f(\mathbf{x})+\lambda g_{1}({\mathbb{E}}[g_{2}(\mathbf{x};\xi)])

which turns the original objective into a nonconvex-concave compositional minimax problem, with composition on the primal variable.

Mixture Weights Estimation
[
14
]

In multiple source domain adaptation, the goal is learn a predictive model for a target domain by finding a mixture weights
\bm{\alpha}
to optimally aggregate empirical risks over
N
data sources, which can be achieved by solving the following minimax problem:

\displaystyle\min_{\bm{\alpha}\in\Delta^{N}}\max_{\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{W}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha(i)f(L_{T}(\mathbf{w})-L_{i}(\mathbf{w}))+\lambda\left\|\bm{\alpha}\right\|_{{\bf M}}

where
\bm{\alpha}
is the mixture weight,
\mathbf{x}
is the model parameter,
L_{T}(\mathbf{w})
is the empirical risk realized by target domain data, and
L_{i}(\mathbf{w})
is the empirical risk realized by
i
th source domain data.
f(z)
is the smooth approximation of the absolute value function.
\left\|\cdot\right\|^{2}_{\bf M}=\bm{\alpha}^{\top}{\bf M}\bm{\alpha}
where
{\bf M}=\diag\left(m_{1},...,m_{N}\right)
. It is a convex-nonconcave minimax problem with composition on the dual variable, and if we relax the norm of
\bm{\alpha}
to the squared norm, it becomes a strongly-convex-nonconcave minimax problem.

Discrepancy Minimization
[
26
,
31
]

In the domain adaptation and empirical estimation of integral probability metrics (IPMs), the goal is to estimate the statistical discrepancy among two distributions or to manipulate a source distribution to align it well with a target distribution, leading to the following problem:

\displaystyle\min_{\mathcal{S}}\sup_{\mathbf{w},\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}}|L_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{w}^{\prime})-L_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{w}^{\prime})|

where
L_{\mathcal{S}}
and
L_{\mathcal{T}}
denote empirical risks over source and target domains, respectively. Here the goal is to find a (empirical) distribution
\mathcal{S}
, such that its discrepancy with target distribution
\mathcal{T}
is minimized, leading to a nonconvex-nonconcave compositional minimax problem.

Despite the well-established convergence theory on non-compositional minimax, there is few literature focusing on its compositional counterpart. The prior works on compositional minimax
[
9
,
21
]
only consider the case of function composition on the primal variable, and hence they only establish the convergence rate in the nonconvex-strongly-concave setting. However, a significant portion of ML applications including aforementioned applications do not satisfy this assumption. The function composition can also be on the dual variable, or on both primal and dual variables, which makes the problem convex-nonconcave or even nonconvex-nonconcave. To fill this gap, we propose a simple yet effective algorithm dubbed as stochastically Corrected stOchastic gradient Descent Ascent (CODA). The proposed algorithm is a variant of the celebrated Stochastic Gradient Descent-Ascent (SGDA) for minimax optimization, with a compositional correction step inspired from
[
1
]
. Depending on the objective structure, the correction step is either applied on the primal update, dual update, or both updates, which yields three algorithm instances: CODA-Primal, CODA-Dual, and CODA-PD (Primal-Dual), respectively. We establish their convergence in nonconvex-(strongly)-concave, (strongly)-convex-nonconcave and weakly-convex-weakly-concave settings correspondingly. Our results demonstrate that intricate compositional structure inherent to these minimax problems does not impose an asymptotic increase to their computational complexity compared to non-compositional variants. This coincides with the findings in compositional minimization setting
[
1
]
.

A summary of prior works and our results on compositional minimax optimization, in different objective settings.

Contributions

The main contributions of the present work are summarized as follows:

•

We introduce a general algorithmic framework, dubbed CODA, for stochastic compositional minimax optimization that encompasses three fundamental scenarios of objective composition: composition on the primal variable (Section
3
), composition on the dual variable (Section
4
), and composition on both variables (Section
5
). We provide a rigorous convergence analysis for various CODA variants tailored to these settings.

•

In composition on the primal variable setting, we examine both nonconvex-strongly-concave and nonconvex-concave cases. Remarkably, our analysis demonstrates that the CODA-Primal algorithm exhibits the same convergence rate as the widely acclaimed SGDA algorithm in their respective non-compositional counterparts.

•

In composition on the dual variable setting, we study strongly-convex-nonconcave and convex-nonconcave cases, and show that our CODA-Dual algorithm can provably converge. This is also the first work that establishes the convergence guarantee on stochastic (compositional) minimax optimization on the general convex-nonconcave objective.

•

In composition on the both variable setting, we study weakly-convex-weakly-concave cases and also provide the convergence guarantee of our CODA-PD algorithm. This work represents the first instance of a compositional minimax algorithm that guarantees convergence in scenarios featuring weakly-convex-weakly-concave objectives.

•

We further propose a variance reduced variant, CODA+, which achieves the best-known convergence rates on nonconvex-strongly-concave and nonconvex-concave compositional minimax problems (Section
6
).

•

Lastly, we conduct experiments across various applications that corroborate the theoretical analysis and demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed algorithms (Section
7
).

2
Related Works

Compositional Optimization

Stochastic compositional optimization has wide applications in risk management and machine learning. Wang et al
[
35
]
is the pioneering work in this field where they proposed the first compositional optimization algorithm, SCGD, and achieved
O(\epsilon^{-4})
rate. An accelerated version of SCGD is then proposed by
[
36
]
, and achieves faster rate
O(\epsilon^{-2.25})
. Tutunov et al
[
34
]
also proposed an accelerated compositional optimization algorithm and achieved the same rate as
[
36
]
. The SOTA rate is achieved by
[
1
]
where their rate matches with the non-compositional setting, demonstrating that solving the compositional minimization problem can be as easy as the non-compositional counterpart.

A line of works also study the compositional minimax problem, particularly in the AUC maximization area. Yuan et al
[
41
]
proposed to optimize a compositional minimax objective, which improves the AUC training results. In their setting, the objective is nonconvex-strongly-concave, and they achieve
O({\epsilon^{-4}})
gradient complexity. Deng et al
[
5
]
studied the mixture weight estimation problem and cast it as a strongly-convex-nonconcave compositional minimax problem. They also proposed an SGDA-based compositional algorithm and achieved
O(\epsilon^{-4})
gradient complexity to find the stationary point. Gao et al
[
9
]
for the first time study the general compositional minimax problem. They consider nonconvex-strongly-concave setting, and achieve
O({\kappa^{4}}{\epsilon^{-4}})
rate. Liu et al
[
21
]
proposed the first variance reduction algorithm for stochastic compositional minimax problem and achieved
O({\kappa^{3}}{\epsilon^{-3}})
rate.

Nonconvex/Nonconcave Minimax optimization

Nonconvex minimax optimization has received increasing attention over the past decade. For nonconvex-strongly-concave minimax optimization,
Lin et al
[
19
]
proved the first non-asymptotic convergence of GDA to
\epsilon
-stationary point of primal function, with the gradient complexity of
O({\kappa^{2}}{\epsilon^{-2}})
. Mahdavinia et al
[
23
]
proved that OGDA and EG method enjoy the same rate in solving nonconvex-strongly-concave problem. Yang et al
[
39
]
developed a single loop smoothed AGDA, which achieved gradient complexity of
O(\kappa\epsilon^{-2})
. Lin et al
[
18
]
proposed an accelerated gradient method based algorithm, and achieved the best known rate
O(\sqrt{\kappa}\epsilon^{-2})
. Zhang et al
[
43
]
proposed triple loop algorithms which also achieved gradient complexity of
O(\sqrt{\kappa}\epsilon^{-2})
using catalyst idea, and inexact proximal point method. These two results match the existing lower bound for deterministic setting
[
16
,
43
,
11
]
. For stochastic setting, Lin et al
[
19
]
proved that SGDA finds
\epsilon
-stationary point of primal function, with the gradient complexity of
O(\kappa^{3}\epsilon^{-4})
respectively. Chen et al
[
2
]
proposed a double loop algorithm and using this idea they achieved gradient complexity of
O(\kappa^{3}\epsilon^{-4})
with fixed batch size. Yang et al
[
39
]
introduced stochastic smoothed AGDA with gradient complexity of
O(\kappa^{2}\epsilon^{-4})
to find stationary point. The significant improvement over SGDA is that stochastic smoothed AGDA uses fixed batch size. Zhang et al
[
45
]
proposed SAPD+, which achieve the SOTA rates of
O(\kappa\epsilon^{-4})
and
O(\kappa^{2}\epsilon^{-3})
under average smoothness and point-wise smoothness assumptions respectively.

For nonconvex-concave optimization, Rafique et al
[
28
]
proposed the pioneering work Proximally Guided Stochastic Mirror Descent Method, which achieves
O({\epsilon^{-6}})
gradient complexity to find the stationary point. Nouiehed et al
[
27
]
presented a double-loop algorithm with
O(\epsilon^{-7})
rate. Lin et al
[
17
]
gave the first proof of GDA and SGDA on nonconvex-concave functions, with
O({\epsilon^{-6}})
rate and
O({\epsilon^{-8}})
rate respectively. Zhang et al
[
42
]
proposed smoothed-GDA and also achieve
O(\epsilon^{-4})
rate. Thekumparampil et al
[
32
]
proposed Proximal Dual Implicit Accelerated Gradient method and achieved the best known rate
O({\epsilon^{-3}})
for nonconvex-concave problem. Kong et al
[
13
]
proposed an accelerated inexact proximal point method and also achieve
O({\epsilon^{-3}})
rate. Lin et al
[
18
]
designed
an algorithm which uses accelerated gradient method as a sub-problem solver, with
O(\epsilon^{-3})
rate. Zhang et al
[
45
]
proposed a variance reduction algorithm SAPD+, which achieve the
O({\epsilon^{-6}})
convergence rate in stochastic setting.

For convex-nonconcave optimization, Xu et al
[
37
]
gave the first convergence proof in this setting, where they consider alternating gradient descent ascent. They achieved
O(\epsilon^{-2})
and
O(\epsilon^{-4})
rate for strongly-convex-nonconcave and convex-nonconcave setting respectively. Later on Deng et al
[
5
]
gave a stochastic algorithm for a special case of compositional strongly-convex-nonconcave minimax problem and achieved
O(\epsilon^{-4})
rate.

For nonconvex-nonconcave optimization, first-order stationary points may not exist if we do not post any further assumptions. Hence, a line of work
[
22
,
6
]
made extra assumptions and developed convergent algorithms. Liu et al
[
22
]
assumed there is a solution of the MVI induced by objective, and gave the first provably convergent algorithm framework for nonconvex-nonconcave minimax problem. Diakonikolas et al
[
6
]
considered the relaxed assumption that only weak MVI solution exists and proposed an EG-based algorithm with convergence guarantee. Zheng et al
[
46
]
consider nonconvex-nonconcave minimax problem with one-side KL condition, and proposed Doubly Smoothed GDA which enjoys
O(\epsilon^{-4})
convergence rate.
Some work also explores to design new convergence measures in general nonconvex-nonconcave minimax problems. Mangoubi et al
[
24
]
proposed a greedy adversarial equilibrium and developed an algorithm with polynomial gradient and hessian complexity to find it.

3
Composition on Primal Variable

We begin by considering the composition in the primal variable:

\displaystyle F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}):=h(\mathbf{x})+{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta\sim\mathcal{S}_{f}}[f({\mathbb{E}}_{\xi\sim\mathcal{S}_{g}}[g(\mathbf{x});\xi],\mathbf{y};\zeta)]-r(\mathbf{y}).

Due to the compositional structure, the objective will become nonconvex in
\mathbf{x}
, and hence we consider two settings: nonconvex-strongly-concave and nonconvex-concave. Our algorithm builds on top of classic SGDA algorithm for minimax optimization. However, due to the compositional structure, we cannot have an unbiased estimation of the gradient with respect to
\mathbf{x}
. To see this, notice the fact:
{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi,\zeta}[\nabla_{1}f(g(\mathbf{x};\xi),\mathbf{y};\zeta)\nabla g(\mathbf{x};\xi)]\neq\nabla_{1}f(g(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{y})\nabla g(\mathbf{x}).

Hence, we borrow the technique from compositional minimization problem
[
1
]
and maintain an auxiliary variable
\mathbf{z}
to approximate inner function
g(\mathbf{x})
. Given a mini-batch
\mathcal{M}^{t}
with size
M
, we update
\mathbf{z}
as:

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}^{t+1}=(1-\beta)\left(\mathbf{z}^{t}+g(\mathbf{x}^{t};\mathcal{M}^{t})-g(\mathbf{x}^{t-1};\mathcal{M}^{t})\right)+\beta g(\mathbf{x}^{t};\mathcal{M}^{t}),

where
g(\mathbf{x};\mathcal{M}^{t}):=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{\xi\in\mathcal{M}^{t}}g(\mathbf{x};\xi)
.
Then, the proposed CODA-Primal algorithm computes the following primal-dual gradients using a mini-batch
\mathcal{B}^{t}
with size
B
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{x}}^{t}=\frac{1}{B}\sum_{(\zeta,\xi)\in\mathcal{B}^{t}}\nabla_{1}f(\mathbf{z}^{t+1},\mathbf{y}^{t};\zeta)\nabla g(\mathbf{x}^{t};\xi)+\nabla h(\mathbf{x}^{t}),\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{y}}^{t}=\frac{1}{B}\sum_{(\zeta,\xi)\in\mathcal{B}^{t}}\nabla_{2}f(\mathbf{z}^{t+1},\mathbf{y}^{t};\zeta)-\nabla r(\mathbf{y}^{t}),

and then performs primal-dual updates on
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{y}
:
{\mathbf{x}}^{t+1}=\mathbf{x}^{t}-\eta_{\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{x}}^{t},\mathbf{y}^{t+1}=\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{Y}}\left(\mathbf{y}^{t}+\eta_{y}\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{y}}^{t}\right)
.

◼ Nonconvex-Strongly-Concave Setting.
We start by providing the convergence rate of CODA-Primal (Algorithm
1
), in the nonconvex-strongly-concave setting. From an algorithmic standpoint, the proposed method is reminiscent of existing methods, but the convergence analysis is more involved due to the minimax, compositional structure, and non-convexity of the objective. We make the following assumptions on the objective:

Assumption 1
.

F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is
\mu
-strongly-concave in
\mathbf{y}
,
\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Assumption 2
.

Domain
\mathcal{Y}
has bounded diameter, i.e.,
\left\|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\right\|\leq D_{\mathcal{Y}}

\forall\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}
.

Assumption 3
.

We make the following assumptions:

1.

f(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{y})
is
L_{f}
smooth,
g(\mathbf{x})
is
L_{g}
smooth , and
F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is
L
smooth.

2.

{\mathbb{E}}[\left\|\nabla g(\mathbf{x};\xi)\right\|^{2}]\leq G_{g}^{2}
and
{\mathbb{E}}[\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{y};\zeta)\right\|^{2}]\leq G_{f}^{2}
.

3.

\max\left\{{\mathbb{E}}\left\|g(\mathbf{x};\xi)-g(\mathbf{x})\right\|^{2},{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\nabla g(\mathbf{x};\xi)-\nabla g(\mathbf{x})\right\|^{2},{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{y};\zeta)-\nabla f(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{y})\right\|^{2}\right\}\leq\sigma^{2}
.

Algorithm 1

CODA-Primal

Input:

({\mathbf{x}}^{0},{\mathbf{y}}^{0})
, stepsizes
\eta_{\mathbf{x}},\eta_{\mathbf{y}}
,
\mathbf{z}^{0}
such that
{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\mathbf{z}^{0}-g(\mathbf{x}^{0})\right\|^{2}\leq\delta
.

for

t=0,\ldots,T

do

Sample a mini-batch
\mathcal{M}^{t}
of
M
data from
\mathcal{S}_{g}
.

\mathbf{z}^{t+1}=(1-\beta)

\left(\mathbf{z}^{t}+\frac{1}{M}\sum_{\xi^{t}\in\mathcal{M}^{t}}\left(g(\mathbf{x}^{t};\xi^{t})-g(\mathbf{x}^{t-1};\xi^{t})\right)\right)+\beta\frac{1}{M}\sum_{\xi^{t}\in\mathcal{M}^{t}}g(\mathbf{x}^{t};\xi^{t})
.

Sample a mini-batch
\mathcal{B}^{t}
of
B
data pair
(\zeta^{t},\xi^{t})
from
\mathcal{S}_{g}\times\mathcal{S}_{f}
.

Compute
\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{x}}^{t}=\frac{1}{B}\sum_{(\zeta,\xi)\in\mathcal{B}^{t}}\nabla_{1}f(\mathbf{z}^{t+1},\mathbf{y}^{t};\zeta)\nabla g(\mathbf{x}^{t};\xi)+\nabla h(\mathbf{x}^{t})
,
\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{y}}^{t}=\frac{1}{B}\sum_{(\zeta,\xi)\in\mathcal{B}^{t}}\nabla_{2}f(\mathbf{z}^{t+1},\mathbf{y}^{t};\zeta)-\nabla r(\mathbf{y}^{t}).

{\mathbf{x}}^{t+1}=\mathbf{x}^{t}-\eta_{\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{x}}^{t}
,
\mathbf{y}^{t+1}=\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{Y}}\left(\mathbf{y}^{t}+\eta_{\mathbf{y}}\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{y}}^{t}\right)
.

end for

Output:

(\hat{\mathbf{x}},\hat{\mathbf{y}})
uniformly sampled from
\left\{({\mathbf{x}}^{t},{\mathbf{y}}^{t})\right\}_{t=1}^{T}
.

Since the objective is nonconvex, we are unable to show the convergence to the global saddle point. Thus, following the standard machinery in nonconvex-concave analysis
[
19
,
32
,
28
]
, we introduce the following primal function to measure the convergence.

Definition 1
.

We define primal function
\Phi(\mathbf{x})=\max_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{Y}}F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
to facilitate our analysis.

We consider the convergence rate to the first order stationary point of
\Phi(\bm{x})
, as advocated in seminal nonconvex-concave minimax literature
[
19
,
28
,
32
]
.

Theorem 1
.

Under Assumptions
1
,
2
,
3
, defining
\kappa:=L/\mu
, for Algorithm
1
, if we choose
\delta=\frac{1}{\kappa}
,
M=B=\Theta\left(\max\left\{\frac{\kappa^{2}L\sigma^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}},1\right\}\right)
,
\beta=\frac{1}{2}
,
\eta_{\mathbf{x}}=\Theta\left(\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}L}\right)
,
\eta_{\mathbf{y}}=\Theta\left(\frac{1}{L}\right)
, then Algorithm
1
guarantees that
\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}\leq\epsilon^{2}

with the gradient complexity bounded by:

\displaystyle O\left(\frac{\kappa^{2}(\Delta_{\Phi}+L^{2}D_{\mathcal{Y}})}{\epsilon^{2}}\max\left\{\frac{\kappa^{2}L\sigma^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}},1\right\}\right),

where
\Delta_{\Phi}:=\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{0})-\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}}\Phi(\mathbf{x})
.

The proof of Theorem
1
is deferred to Appendix
C.1
. Here we achieve an
O(\frac{\kappa^{4}}{\epsilon^{4}})
rate, which is the same as the rate of SGDA on non-compositional objective
[
19
]
. This suggests that we can solve nonconvex-strongly-concave compositional minimax problems as readily as non-compositional cases. The most relevant work is
[
9
]
, where they also achieve an
O(\frac{\kappa^{4}}{\epsilon^{4}})
rate. An even faster rate is attainable through the use of variance reduction techniques, as will be shown and discussed in Section
6
. As a final remark, we need to choose a good initialization of
\mathbf{z}^{0}
such that it is close to
g(\mathbf{x}^{0})
, i.e.,
{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\mathbf{z}^{0}-g(\mathbf{x}^{0})\right\|^{2}\leq\frac{1}{\kappa}
. This will take
\kappa\sigma^{2}
samples drawn from
\mathcal{S}_{g}
.

◼ Nonconvex-Concave Setting.
We now turn to the analysis in nonconvex-concave setting.

Assumption 4
.

F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is concave in
\mathbf{y}
,
\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
h(\mathbf{x})
is
G_{h}
Lipschitz.

Since
F
is merely concave in
\mathbf{y}
, primal function
\Phi(\mathbf{x})
is not smooth anymore, and its gradient is not a viable quantity to measure the convergence. Following the convention
[
19
]
, we consider the gradient of Moreau Envelope of primal function as the convergence measure.

Definition 2

(Moreau Envelope)
.

A function
\Phi_{\rho}(\mathbf{w})
is the
\rho
-Moreau envelope of a function
\Phi
if
\Phi_{\rho}(\mathbf{w}):=\min_{\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}}\{\Phi(\mathbf{w}^{\prime})+\frac{1}{2\rho}\|\mathbf{w}^{\prime}-\mathbf{w}\|^{2}\}
.

Theorem 2
.

Under Assumptions
2
,
3
, and
4
, for Algorithm
1
, if we choose
\delta=O(1)
,
B=\Theta\left(1\right)
,
M=\Theta\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}L^{4}D^{2}_{\mathcal{Y}}}{\epsilon^{4}}\right)
,
\beta=\frac{1}{2}
,
\eta_{\mathbf{y}}=\Theta\left(\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{L\sigma^{2}}\right)
,
\eta_{\mathbf{x}}=\Theta\left(\frac{\epsilon^{6}}{LD_{\mathcal{Y}}\sigma^{2}(G_{h}+G_{f}G_{g})}\right)
, then Algorithm
1
guarantees that
\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\nabla\Phi_{1/2L}(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}\leq\epsilon^{2}

with gradient complexity bounded by

\displaystyle O\left(\max\left\{\frac{L^{3}\sigma^{2}D^{2}_{\mathcal{Y}}(G_{h}+G_{f}G_{g})\Delta_{\hat{\Phi}}}{\epsilon^{8}},\frac{L\Delta_{\Phi}}{\epsilon^{2}}\right\}\right),

where
\Delta_{\Phi}:=\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{0})-\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}}\Phi(\mathbf{x})
,
\Delta_{\hat{\Phi}}:=\Phi_{1/2L}(\mathbf{x}^{0})-\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}}\Phi_{1/2L}(\mathbf{x})
.

The proof of Theorem
2
is deferred to Appendix
C.2
.
Unlike the proof technique used in compositional minimization or minimax optimization
[
1
,
9
]
that only considers potential function with second moment of inner function estimation error
{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\mathbf{z}^{t}-g(\mathbf{x}^{t-1})\right\|^{2}
, in nonconvex-concave minimax setting we rely on the potential function with both first and second moment of :
\Psi^{t}=\Phi_{1/2L}(\mathbf{x}^{t})+O(\frac{\eta_{\mathbf{x}}}{(2\beta-\beta^{2})}G_{g}^{2}L_{f}^{2}){\mathbb{E}}\left\|\mathbf{z}^{t}-g(\mathbf{x}^{t-1})\right\|^{2}+O(\frac{\eta_{\mathbf{x}}L^{2}D_{\mathbf{y}}}{\beta}){\mathbb{E}}\left\|\mathbf{z}^{t}-g(\mathbf{x}^{t-1})\right\|
.

We recover
O(\epsilon^{-8})
gradient complexity, the same as SGDA on the non-compositional nonconvex-concave objective. Notice that we also need
T\cdot M=O(\epsilon^{-12})
zeroth order evaluations for maintaining
\mathbf{z}
variable, but zeroth order evaluation (i.e., inference) is usually considered much cheaper than gradient evaluation, and can be implemented efficiently in practice. This is also the first convergence result for nonconvex-concave compositional minimax optimization.

4
Composition on Dual Variable

In this section, we consider the setting where the composition happens on the dual variable:

\displaystyle F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}):=h(\mathbf{x})+{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta\sim\mathcal{S}_{f}}[f(\mathbf{x},{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi\sim\mathcal{S}_{g}}[g(\mathbf{y});\xi];\zeta)]-r(\mathbf{y}).

In this setting the objective is possibly nonconcave in
\mathbf{y}
. We hence consider strongly-convex-nonconcave and convex-nonconcave settings to establish the convergence rate. The algorithm employed is CODA-Dual, which is akin to CODA-Primal, but instead utilizes a compositional correction step on the dual variable as shown in Algorithm
2
. The little difference is that, we have a
\alpha_{t}\mathbf{x}^{t}
term which serves as a regularizer. When the objective is strongly convex in
\mathbf{x}
, we will set
\alpha_{t}
to be
0
. When it is merely convex in
\mathbf{x}
, we will set
\alpha_{t}
to be a small value to make the objective strongly convex. This technique was used in vanilla convex-nonconcave minimax problem
[
37
]
.

Algorithm 2

CODA-Dual

Input:

({\mathbf{x}}^{0},{\mathbf{y}}^{0})
,
\left\{\alpha_{t}\right\}
, stepsizes
\eta_{\mathbf{x}},\eta_{\mathbf{y}}
,
\mathbf{z}^{0}
such that
{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\mathbf{z}^{0}-g(\mathbf{x}^{0})\right\|^{2}\leq\delta
.

for

t=0,...,T-1

do

Sample a mini-batch
\mathcal{M}^{t}
of
M
data from
\mathcal{S}_{g}
.

\mathbf{z}^{t+1}=(1-\beta)\left(\mathbf{z}^{t}+\frac{1}{M}\sum_{\xi\in\mathcal{M}^{t}}\left(g(\mathbf{y}^{t};\xi)-g(\mathbf{y}^{t-1};\xi)\right)\right)+\beta\frac{1}{M}\sum_{\xi\in\mathcal{M}^{t}}g(\mathbf{y}^{t};\xi)
.

Sample a mini-batch
\mathcal{B}^{t}
of
B
data pair
(\zeta,\xi)
from
\mathcal{S}_{f}\times\mathcal{S}_{g}
,

Compute
\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{x}}^{t}=\frac{1}{B}\sum_{(\zeta,\xi)\in\mathcal{B}^{t}}\nabla_{1}f(\mathbf{x}^{t},\mathbf{z}^{t+1};\zeta)+\nabla h(\mathbf{x}^{t})+\alpha_{t}\mathbf{x}^{t}
,
{\mathbf{g}}_{\mathbf{y}}^{t}=\frac{1}{B}\sum_{(\zeta,\xi)\in\mathcal{B}^{t}}\nabla_{2}f(\mathbf{x}^{t},\mathbf{z}^{t+1};\zeta)\nabla g(\mathbf{y}^{t};\xi)-\nabla r(\mathbf{y}^{t})
.

{\mathbf{x}}^{t+1}=\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{X}}\left(\mathbf{x}^{t}-\eta_{\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{x}}^{t}\right)
,
\mathbf{y}^{t+1}=\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{Y}}\left(\mathbf{y}^{t}+\eta_{\mathbf{y}}\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{y}}^{t}\right)
.

end for

Output:

(\hat{\mathbf{x}},\hat{\mathbf{y}})
uniformly sampled from
\left\{({\mathbf{x}}^{t},{\mathbf{y}}^{t})\right\}_{t=1}^{T}
.

◼ Strongly-convex-Nonconcave Setting.

We first present the convergence result of CODA-Dual in the strongly-convex-nonconcave setting by making the following standard assumptions.

Assumption 5
.

F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is
\mu
-strongly-convex in
\mathbf{x}
,
\forall\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{Y}
and
L
smooth.

Assumption 6
.

We make the following assumptions:

1.

f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})
is
L_{f}
smooth,
g(\mathbf{y})
is
L_{g}
smooth, and
F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is
L
smooth.

2.

{\mathbb{E}}[\left\|\nabla g(\mathbf{y};\xi)\right\|^{2}]\leq G^{2}_{g}
and
{\mathbb{E}}[\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z};\zeta)\right\|^{2}]\leq G^{2}_{f}
.

3.

\max\left\{{\mathbb{E}}\left\|g(\mathbf{y};\xi)-g(\mathbf{y})\right\|^{2},{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\nabla g(\mathbf{y};\xi)-\nabla g(\mathbf{y})\right\|^{2},{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z};\zeta)-\nabla f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})\right\|^{2}\right\}\leq\sigma^{2}
.

Assumption 7
.

Domain
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
have bounded diameter, i.e.,
\left\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right\|\leq D_{\mathcal{X}}
and
\left\|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\right\|\leq D_{\mathcal{Y}}
,
\forall\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}
. We use
D
to denote
D=\max\left\{D_{\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{Y}}\right\}
.

Assumption 8
.

There exists a constant
F_{\max}
such that
\max_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X},\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{Y}}F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\leq F_{\max}
.

Next, we consider the following convergence measure:

Definition 3

(Convergence Measure
[
37
]
)
.

Given two parameters,
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{y}
, we define the following quantity as a stationary gap
\nabla G(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{\eta_{\mathbf{x}}}\left(\mathbf{x}-\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{X}}\left(\mathbf{x}-\eta_{\mathbf{x}}\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right)\right)\\
\frac{1}{\eta_{\mathbf{y}}}\left(\mathbf{y}-\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{Y}}\left(\mathbf{y}+\eta_{\mathbf{y}}\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right)\right)\end{pmatrix}~.

Given the nonconcave nature of
F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
, we are only able to show the convergence to a stationary point. Definition
3
measures the stationarity given parameter pair
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
by examining how much the parameter will change if we run one step projected gradient descent-ascent on them.
The widely employed convergence measure
primal function

\left\|\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x})\right\|
does not apply here due to non-concavity.

Theorem 3
.

Under Assumptions
5
-
8
, if we choose
M=\Theta\left(\max\left\{\frac{\kappa^{3}L\sigma^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}}1\right\}\right),B=\Theta\left(\max\left\{\frac{\kappa^{2}L_{f}^{2}\sigma^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}},1\right\}\right),\beta=0.1,\eta_{\mathbf{x}}=\Theta\left(\min\left\{\frac{1}{L^{2}},\frac{\mu}{L^{2}_{f}}\right\}\right),\eta_{\mathbf{y}}=\Theta\left(\frac{\eta_{\mathbf{x}}}{\kappa^{2}}\right)
,
\delta=O(1)
, and
\alpha_{t}=0
, then Algorithm
2
guarantees that
\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\nabla G(\mathbf{x}^{t},\mathbf{y}^{t})\right\|^{2}\leq\epsilon^{2}

with the gradient complexity bounded by:

\displaystyle O\left(\max\left\{\frac{\kappa^{2}L^{2}_{f}\sigma^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}},1\right\}\cdot\frac{\kappa^{3}F_{\max}}{\epsilon^{2}}\right).

The proof of Theorem
3
is deferred to Appendix
D.1
. The heart of our proof is constructing a two-level potential function, together with controlling the iterate difference of auxiliary variable
\left\|\mathbf{z}^{t+1}-\mathbf{z}^{t}\right\|^{2}
, so that we can derive the proper descent inequality. Here we achieve
O(\epsilon^{-4})
rate, worse than
deterministic
non-compositional setting
[
37
]
by
O(\epsilon^{-2})
. The most relevant work is
[
5
]
, where they also solve a strongly-convex-nonconcave compositional minimax problem. The main difference is that their objective has a linear coupling of primal and dual variables, while we consider a more general objective. To guarantee the convergence, the ratio of primal stepsize to dual stepsize needs to be
\kappa^{2}
, due to the asymmetry nature of our objective.

Reducing SC-NC to NC-SC Problem.
One may argue that finding stationary point of an strongly-convex-nonconcave (SC-NC) minimax problem can be solved by an algorithm that can find that of an nonconvex-strongly-concave (NC-SC) problem. However, in the SC-NC problem, we must consider the gradient norm of
F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
as the convergence measure, while all existing NC-SC compositional minimax algorithm only has convergence guarantee in terms of gradient norm of primal function
\Phi(\mathbf{x})
. It is well-known that (
[
17
]
, Proposition 4.11), translating an
\epsilon
stationary point in term of
\Phi(\mathbf{x})
to
\epsilon
stationary point in term of
F
will need
O(\epsilon^{-2})
more stochastic gradient evaluations, hence if we simply use the result from Theorem 1, we will need another sub-rountine to translate the stationary point, which is less preferred than our single loop algorithm CODA-Dual.

◼ Convex-Nonconcave Setting

In this section, we present the convergence result of CODA-Dual when the objective is merely convex in
\mathbf{x}
.

Assumption 9
.

F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is convex in
\mathbf{x}
,
\forall\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{Y}
.

Theorem 4
.

Under Assumptions
6
-
9
, if we choose
M=\Theta\left(\max\left\{\frac{L^{6}L_{f}^{2}D_{\mathcal{X}}^{4}\sigma^{2}}{\epsilon^{4}},1\right\}\right)
,
B=\Theta\left(\max\left\{\frac{L^{3}L_{f}^{2}D_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\sigma^{2}}{\epsilon^{4}},1\right\}\right)
,
\delta=O(1)
,
\beta=0.1
,

\eta_{\mathbf{x}}=\Theta\left(\frac{1}{L^{2}}\right)
,
\eta_{\mathbf{y}}=\Theta\left(\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{L^{4}D_{\mathcal{X}}}\right)
, and
\alpha_{t}=\Theta\left(\frac{\epsilon}{LD_{\mathcal{X}}}\right)
, then for Algorithm
2
it is guaranteed that
\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\nabla G(\mathbf{x}^{t},\mathbf{y}^{t})\right\|^{2}\leq\epsilon^{2}
holds
with the gradient complexity of

\displaystyle O\left(\max\left\{\frac{L^{3}L_{f}^{2}D_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\sigma^{2}}{\epsilon^{4}},1\right\}\cdot\frac{F_{\max}D_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}L^{4}}{\epsilon^{4}}\right).

The proof of Theorem
4
is deferred to Appendix
D.3
.
Here we achieve an
O(\epsilon^{-8})
convergence rate to the stationary point of
F
in the convex-nonconcave setting. This is also the first convergence rate for stochastic convex-nonconcave minimax optimization.

5
Composition on Primal and Dual Variables

In this section, we consider composition on both variables

\displaystyle F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}):=h(\mathbf{x})+{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta\sim\mathcal{S}_{f}}[f({\mathbb{E}}_{\xi\sim\mathcal{S}_{g}}[g(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y};\xi)];\zeta)]-r(\mathbf{y}).

In this setting, we may lose convexity and concavity on primal and dual variables, so the problem becomes nonconvex-nonconcave minimax optimization. In general, if we make no assumptions (except smoothness), the first-order stationarity may not be a viable quantity to control convergence, and a more sophisticated optimality measure is necessary, e.g., greedy adversarial equilibrium
[
24
]
. In this paper, following a branch of nonconvex-nonconcave optimization literature
[
22
,
6
]
, we make a mild assumption on our nonconvex-nonconcave objective
F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
, that there exists a point that is the solution of Minty Variational Inequality induced by
F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
.

Assumption 10

(Existence of Solution for Minty Variational Inequality)
.

There exists a
(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{y}^{*})\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
, such that the following Minty variational inequality induced by
\nabla F
can hold:

\displaystyle\left\langle\nabla F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),\,(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{y}^{*})\right\rangle\geq 0,\forall(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}.

◼ Weakly-convex-weakly-concave Setting.

Relying on reduction introduced in
[
22
]
, we can break solving a weakly-convex-weakly-concave minimax problem into solving a bunch of strongly monotone variational inequality problems. Hence we first derive the following convergence of CODA-SCSC on solving Strongly Monotone VI.

Lemma 1
.

Assume that
F_{k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is
\mu
strongly convex in
\mathbf{x}
and
\mu
strongly concave in
\mathbf{y}
, and
L
smooth, and
\max\left\{D_{\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{Y}}\right\}\leq D
, then if we run Algorithm
3
on
F_{k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
with
\eta_{\mathbf{x}}=\eta_{\mathbf{y}}=\eta
, the solution
\hat{\mathbf{w}}=(\hat{\mathbf{x}},\hat{\mathbf{y}})
returned by algorithm guarantees that
\max_{\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{W}}{\mathbb{E}}[\nabla F_{k}(\hat{\mathbf{w}})^{\top}(\hat{\mathbf{w}}-\mathbf{w})]\leq\epsilon

with gradient complexity
O\left(\left(D+\frac{1}{\eta}\right)\frac{L^{2}\sigma^{2}}{\mu^{2}\eta\epsilon}\ln\left(\frac{D+1/\eta}{\epsilon}\right)\right).

Algorithm 3

CODA-SCSC(
F,\mathbf{x}^{0},\mathbf{y}^{0},T
)

Input:

({\mathbf{x}}^{0},{\mathbf{y}}^{0})
,
\mathbf{z}^{0}
such that
{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\mathbf{z}^{0}-g(\mathbf{x}^{0})\right\|^{2}\leq\delta
.

for

t=0,...,T-1

do

Sample a mini-batch
\mathcal{M}^{t}
of
M
data from
\mathcal{S}_{g}
.

\mathbf{z}^{t+1}=(1-\beta)\left(\mathbf{z}^{t}+\frac{1}{M}\sum_{\xi\in\mathcal{M}^{t}}g(\mathbf{x}^{t},\mathbf{y}^{t};\xi)-g(\mathbf{x}^{t-1},\mathbf{y}^{t-1};\xi)\right)+\beta\frac{1}{M}\sum_{\xi\in\mathcal{M}^{t}}g(\mathbf{x}^{t},\mathbf{y}^{t};\xi)
.

Sample a mini-batch
\mathcal{B}^{t}
of
B
data pair
(\zeta^{t},\xi^{t})
from
\mathcal{S}_{f}\times\mathcal{S}_{g}
.

Compute
\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{x}}^{t}=\frac{1}{B}\sum_{(\zeta,\xi)\in\mathcal{B}^{t}}\nabla f(\mathbf{z}^{t+1};\zeta)\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}g(\mathbf{x}^{t},\mathbf{y}^{t};\xi)+\nabla h(\mathbf{x}^{t})+\frac{2}{\gamma}\left(\mathbf{x}^{t}-\mathbf{x}^{0}\right)
,
\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{y}}^{t}=\frac{1}{B}\sum_{(\zeta,\xi)\in\mathcal{B}^{t}}\nabla f(\mathbf{z}^{t+1};\zeta)\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}g(\mathbf{x}^{t},\mathbf{y}^{t};\xi)-\nabla r(\mathbf{y}^{t})-\frac{2}{\gamma}\left(\mathbf{y}^{t}-\mathbf{y}^{0}\right)
.

{\mathbf{x}}^{t+1}=\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{X}}\left(\mathbf{x}^{t}-\eta_{\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{x}}^{t}\right)
,
\mathbf{y}^{t+1}=\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{Y}}\left(\mathbf{y}^{t}+\eta_{\mathbf{y}}\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{y}}^{t}\right)
.

end for

Output:

(\mathbf{x}^{T},\mathbf{y}^{T})
.

Algorithm 4

CODA-PD

Input:

({\mathbf{x}}_{0},{\mathbf{y}}_{0})
,
\gamma
, stepsizes
\eta_{\mathbf{x}},\eta_{\mathbf{y}}
, weights
\left\{\theta^{k}\right\}_{k=0}^{K-1}
.

for

k=0,\ldots,K-1

do

Construct
F_{k}=F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})+\frac{1}{2\gamma}\left(\left\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}_{k}\right\|^{2}-\left\|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{y}_{k}\right\|^{2}\right)

(\mathbf{x}_{k+1},\mathbf{y}_{k+1})=\texttt{CODA-SCSC}(F_{k},\mathbf{x}_{k},\mathbf{y}_{k},T_{k})

end for

Output:

(\hat{\mathbf{x}},\hat{\mathbf{y}})
sampled from
\left\{({\mathbf{x}}_{k},{\mathbf{y}}_{k})\right\}_{k=0}^{K-1}
with
\Pr\left((\hat{\mathbf{x}},\hat{\mathbf{y}})=({\mathbf{x}}_{k},{\mathbf{y}}_{k})\right)=\frac{\theta^{k}}{\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}\theta^{k}}
.

Assumption 11
.

We make the following assumptions:

1.

F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is is
L
smooth,
\rho
-weakly-convex in
\mathbf{x}
, and
\rho
-weakly-concave in
\mathbf{y}
.

2.

f(\mathbf{z})
is
L_{f}
smooth and
g(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is
L_{g}
smooth.

3.

{\mathbb{E}}[\left\|\nabla g(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y};\xi)\right\|^{2}]\leq G^{2}_{g}
and
{\mathbb{E}}[\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{z};\zeta)\right\|^{2}]\leq G^{2}_{f}
.

4.

\max\left\{{\mathbb{E}}\left\|g(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y};\xi)-g(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\|^{2},{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\nabla g(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y};\xi)-\nabla g(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\|^{2},{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{z};\zeta)-\nabla f(\mathbf{z})\right\|^{2}\right\}\leq\sigma^{2}
.

Definition 4

(Convergence Measure
[
22
]
)
.

A solution
\mathbf{w}=(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is
\epsilon
stationary point of problem (
1
) if there exists
\bar{\mathbf{w}}=(\bar{\mathbf{x}},\bar{\mathbf{y}})
such that
\left\|\mathbf{w}-\bar{\mathbf{w}}\right\|\leq c\epsilon,dist\left(0,\partial(F(\bar{\mathbf{x}},\bar{\mathbf{y}})+\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}}(\bar{\mathbf{x}},\bar{\mathbf{y}}))\right)\leq\epsilon
for some
c>0
.

Theorem 5
.

Under Assumptions
7
,
10
and
11
, if we choose
B=\Theta\left(\max\left\{\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}},1\right\}\right),\beta=1-\frac{\mu}{16G^{2}_{g}L_{f}},\eta_{\mathbf{x}}=\eta_{\mathbf{y}}=\Theta\left(\frac{1}{L^{2}}\right)
,
\delta=O(1)
,
\theta^{k}=(k+1)^{\alpha}
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
\gamma=\frac{1}{\rho}
, then Algorithm
2
returns
\epsilon
stationary solution
(\hat{\mathbf{x}},\hat{\mathbf{y}})
with the gradient complexity bounded by:

\displaystyle O\left(\left(D+L^{2}\right)\frac{\rho^{2}DL^{4}\sigma^{2}}{\epsilon^{4}}\log\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right)\right).

The proof is deferred to Appendix
E
. Here we achieved an
\tilde{O}(\epsilon^{-4})
rate. The most relevant work is
[
22
]
, demonstrating an
\tilde{O}(\epsilon^{-2})
rate using (deterministic) gradient descent ascent as inner problem solver.

6
Faster Rates with Variance Reduction

In this section, our goal is to explore the possibility of achieving faster rates. If we assume individual or point-wise smoothness of
f
and
g
, i.e., smoothness of
f(g(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{y};\zeta)
and
g(\mathbf{x};\xi)
, then the natural idea would be employing variance reduction minimax algorithms, such as VR-SAPD
[
45
]
, combined with our compositional correction steps. Unfortunately, this integration solely does not lead to an improved rate since the main bottleneck comes from the estimation error of inner function
g(\mathbf{x})
, i.e.,
{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\mathbf{z}^{t+1}-g(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}
. This observation raises the following question: can we accelerate the convergence of estimation error of inner function to entail a faster convergence rate overall? Inspired by Spider
[
7
]
, we propose to use the following
variance reduced inner function estimation
when maintaining
\mathbf{z}
variable:

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}^{t+1}=(1-\beta)(\mathbf{z}^{t}+g(\mathbf{x}^{t};\mathcal{I}^{t}_{\mathbf{z}})-g(\mathbf{x}^{t-1};\mathcal{I}^{t}_{\mathbf{z}}))+\beta g^{t-1},

where
g^{t}=g^{t-1}+g(\mathbf{x}^{t};\mathcal{I}^{t}_{\mathbf{z}})-g(\mathbf{x}^{t-1};\mathcal{I}^{t}_{\mathbf{z}})
and every
\tau
iterations, we use a large mini-batch to update
g^{t}
:
g^{t}=g(\mathbf{x}^{t};\mathcal{B}^{t}_{\mathbf{z}})
.

It can be shown, the gap
{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\mathbf{z}^{t+1}-g(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}
converges faster than standard compositional optimization as in
[
1
]
. Equipped with this technique, we propose our CODA+ algorithm depicted in Algorithm
5
, Appendix
B
.

Nonconvex-strongly-concave Setting.
The following theorem establishes the convergence:

Assumption 12
.

We make the following assumptions:

1.

f(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{y};\zeta)
is
L_{f}
smooth,
g(\mathbf{x};\xi)
is
L_{g}
smooth for any
\zeta\in\mathcal{S}_{f},\xi\in\mathcal{S}_{g}
, and
F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is
L
smooth.

2.

{\mathbb{E}}[\left\|\nabla g(\mathbf{x};\xi)\right\|^{2}]\leq G_{g}^{2}
and
{\mathbb{E}}[\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{y};\zeta)\right\|^{2}]\leq G_{f}^{2}
.

3.

\max\left\{{\mathbb{E}}\left\|g(\mathbf{x};\xi)-g(\mathbf{x})\right\|^{2},{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\nabla g(\mathbf{x};\xi)-\nabla g(\mathbf{x})\right\|^{2},{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{y};\zeta)-\nabla f(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{y})\right\|^{2}\right\}\leq\sigma^{2}
.

Theorem 6
.

Under Assumptions
1
,
2
,
12
, for Algorithm
5
, if we choose
\delta=O(\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{L})
,
B_{\tau}=\Theta\left(\frac{L^{2}\sigma^{2}}{\mu\epsilon^{2}}\right)
,
\tau=\sqrt{\frac{B_{\tau}}{\kappa}}
,
B=\sqrt{\kappa B_{\tau}}
, and
\eta_{\mathbf{x}}=\Theta\left(\frac{1}{L}\right)
,
\eta_{\mathbf{y}}=\Theta\left(\frac{1}{L}\right)
,
\mu_{\mathbf{x}}=2L
, then it guarantees that
\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\nabla\Phi_{1/2L}(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}\leq\epsilon^{2}

with the gradient complexity bounded by:

\displaystyle O\left(\frac{\kappa^{2}{L}^{1.5}\Delta_{\Phi}\sigma}{\epsilon^{3}}\right).

The proof of Theorem
6
is deferred to Appendix
F.1
. Here we achieve
O\left(\frac{\kappa^{2}{L}^{1.5}}{\epsilon^{3}}\right)
rate for finding the stationary point of Moreau envelope of primal function. Notice that once we find
\epsilon
stationary point of Moreau envelope, there will be an efficient algorithm that takes only
\tilde{O}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
stochastic gradient complexity to find
\epsilon
stationary point of primal function, as indicated in
[
45
]
.
This rate is faster than previous SOTA
[
20
]
, by
O(\kappa)
factor. Compared to non-compositional minimax optimization, the best-known rate is
O\left(\frac{\kappa^{2}{L}}{\epsilon^{3}}\right)
, so our result almost recovers the current non-compositional minimax SOTA rate. As a last final, we need to choose a good initialization of
\mathbf{z}^{0}
such that
{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\mathbf{z}^{0}-g(\mathbf{x}^{0})\right\|^{2}\leq O({\epsilon^{2}}/{L})
. This will take
O({L\sigma^{2}}/{\epsilon^{2}})
samples drawn from
\mathcal{S}_{g}
.

◼ Nonconvex-concave Setting.
In the nonconvex-concave setting, we consider CODA-Primal+ on the following augmented strongly concave objective with strongly concavity parameter
\mu=\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{LD^{2}_{\mathcal{Y}}}
:

\displaystyle\tilde{F}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}):=F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{LD^{2}_{\mathcal{Y}}}\left\|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{y}_{0}\right\|^{2}.

(2)

Theorem 7
.

Under Assumptions
2
,
4
and
12
, if we run Algorithm
5
on
\tilde{F}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
defined in (
2
), and use the same parameter choice as in Theorem
6
with
\mu=\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{LD^{2}_{\mathcal{Y}}}
, then it guarantees that
\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\nabla\Phi_{1/2L}(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}\leq\epsilon^{2}

with the gradient complexity bounded by
O\left(\frac{{L}^{5.5}\Delta_{\Phi}\sigma}{\epsilon^{7}}\right).

The proof of Theorem
7
is deferred to Appendix
F.2
.
This result improves the original
O(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{8}})
rate in Theorem
2
. Faster rate is possible, if we can develop an algorithm which converges at the rate of
O(\frac{\kappa}{\epsilon^{4}})
in the strongly concave setting, and choose
\mu=\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{LD^{2}_{\mathcal{Y}}}
which will yield
O(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{6}})
rate in the merely concave setting. Such an algorithm can be possibly achieved by modifying SAPD
[
45
]
, combined with our variance-reduced inner function estimation, which we leave as future work.

7
Experiment

In this section, we conduct experiemnts to verify the effectiveness of our proposed algorithms. In primal composition setting, we run CODA-Primal on AUC optimization and robust MAML tasks, where we are tasked with solving nonconvex-strongly-concave and nonconvex-concave compositional minimax problems, respectively. In dual composition setting, we run CODA-Dual on mixture weights estimation problem, where we need to solve strongly-convex-nonconcave compositional minimax problem. The additional results are provided in
A
.

Figure 2
:
Testing AUC comparison of CODA and SCGDA on four benchmarks under imratio
=10\%

Deep AUC Maximization.

To evaluate our CODA-Primal algorithm on nonconvex-strongly-concave objective, we consider deep AUC maximization task
[
41
]
. We choose four benchmark image classification datasets: CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, CATvsDog, and STL-10. We generate imbalanced binary versions of these datasets with imbalanced ratios of
1\%,10\%
, and
30\%
, following the positive examples’ proportion to total training samples, following the work
[
41
]
. Employing ResNet20 as our prediction network and setting the weight decay to 1e-4, we optimize for AUC over 100 epochs with a batch size of 128. For all benchmark datasets, we conduct experiments under three different random seeds, calculating mean values and standard deviations. Figure
1
illustrates the convergence and AUC scores of CODA-Primal across these four datasets at different imbalance ratios. We also compare CODA with SCGDA
[
9
]
on testing AUC performance, and the results are shown in Figure
2
.

Task-agnostic Robust MAML.

In this experiment we apply CODA-Primal algorithm to a nonconvex-concave objective. by considering task-agnostic robust MAML
[
4
]
. We use the Omniglot dataset
[
15
]
, 1623 handwritten characters from 50 alphabets, to learn a task-robust meta-model to enhance performance and adaptability across diverse task distributions. We define tasks as
N
-way,
K
-shot classification problems, each involving characters from a single alphabet, and use a 4-layer CNN
[
8
]
. Our approach involves meta-training on 25 alphabets and meta-testing on 20 different alphabets, following the same data splits in meta-learning evaluations
[
33
]
. After 60,000 meta-training iterations, we evaluate our model on 5,000 tasks from meta-test alphabets and 5,000 tasks from meta-training alphabets. The detailed accuracies, particularly when
N=5
and
N=20
, are shown in Table
2
, demonstrating the effectiveness of CODA in nonconvex-concave compositional minimax problem.

Mixture Weights Estimation in Multi-source Domain Adaptation.

In this experiment we test our CODA-Dual algorithm on strongly-convex-nonconcave objective. We consider mixture weight estimation task
[
14
,
5
]
. Given multiple source domains and a target domains, our goal is to find a good mixture of sources such that learning on it can yield a good target model. We use the CIFAR-10 dataset, divided into 4 different groups. For Groups 1, 2, 3, each group contains 5 domains drawing data from distinct classes, e.g., domains in Group 1 only draw data from first 3 classes of CIFAR10, and Group 4 contains 5 domains drawing data from classes in Groups 1 and 2 equally. In each test, we treat domains in one group as target and rest as sources. Hence the training involves 15 source domains (5 in each of three groups), with 1500 samples per domain. Following
[
25
]
, our evaluation includes two baselines: (i) target-only: training only on limited target domain data, (ii) average(equal)-weight: equal weight training across all source domains. Table
3
shows CODA-Dual outperforms in test accuracy, effectively identifying source domains with mixture weight
\bm{\alpha}
. Results are averaged over ten random runs.

8
Conclusion

In this paper, we study the compositional minimax problem, with three different settings: composition on primal variable, on dual variable, and on both variables. We give simple yet effective provable algorithms for three settings. Additionally, we give a variance reduction algorithm which achieves SOTA convergence rates. Extensive experimental results across different applications corroborate our theoretical findings and effectiveness of the proposed methods.

Experiments Details

Deep AUC Maximization:

In a binary classification task where the label
y\in\{-1,+1\}
and the model parameter
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the model prediction on sample
\mathbf{x}
is
f(\mathbf{w};\mathbf{x}_{i})
. The objective is formulated as a minimax problem:

\displaystyle\min_{\mathbf{w},a,b}\max_{\theta\in\Omega}\Phi(\mathbf{w}-\alpha\nabla L(\mathbf{w}),a,b,\theta)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi(\mathbf{w}-\alpha\nabla L(\mathbf{w}),a,b,\theta;\mathbf{x}_{i},y_{i})

where
L(\mathbf{w})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell(\mathbf{w};\mathbf{x}_{i},y_{i})
and

\displaystyle\phi(\mathbf{w},a,b,\theta;\mathbf{x}_{i},y_{i})=

\displaystyle(1-p)(f(\mathbf{w};\mathbf{x}_{i})-a)^{2}\mathbb{I}[y_{i}=1]+p(f(\mathbf{w};\mathbf{x}_{i})-b)^{2}\mathbb{I}[y_{i}=-1]

\displaystyle+2\theta\left(pf(\mathbf{w};\mathbf{x}_{i})\mathbb{I}[y_{i}=-1]-(1-p)f(\mathbf{w};\mathbf{x}_{i})\mathbb{I}[y_{i}=1]\right)

\displaystyle+2pf(\mathbf{w};\mathbf{x}_{i})\mathbb{I}[y_{i}=1]-2(1-p)f(\mathbf{w};\mathbf{x}_{i})\mathbb{I}[y_{i}=-1]-p(1-p)\theta^{2}

At time t, sample a minibatch
(\mathbf{x}_{1},y_{1}),...,(\mathbf{x}_{B},y_{B})
from the training set.

First, update the auxiliary variable:

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}^{t+1}=

\displaystyle(1-\beta^{t})\left(\mathbf{z}^{t}+\left(\mathbf{w}^{t}-\alpha\frac{1}{B}\sum_{i=1}^{B}\nabla\ell(\mathbf{w}^{t};\mathbf{x}_{i},y_{i})\right)-\left(\mathbf{w}^{t-1}-\alpha\frac{1}{B}\sum_{i=1}^{B}\nabla\ell(\mathbf{w}^{t-1};\mathbf{x}_{i},y_{i})\right)\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\beta^{t}\left(\mathbf{w}^{t}-\alpha\frac{1}{B}\sum_{i=1}^{B}\nabla\ell(\mathbf{w}^{t};\mathbf{x}_{i},y_{i})\right)

Then, sample another minibatch
(\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{1},\tilde{y}_{1}),...,(\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{B},\tilde{y}_{B})
and compute:

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{w}}^{t}=\frac{1}{B}\sum_{i=1}^{B}\nabla\phi(\mathbf{z}^{t+1},a^{t},b^{t},\theta^{t};\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{i},\tilde{y}_{i}),

\displaystyle\ g_{a}^{t}=\frac{1}{B}\sum_{i=1}^{B}\nabla_{a}\phi(\mathbf{z}^{t+1},a^{t},b^{t},\theta^{t};\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{i},\tilde{y}_{i}),

\displaystyle g_{b}^{t}=\frac{1}{B}\sum_{i=1}^{B}\nabla_{b}\phi(\mathbf{z}^{t+1},a^{t},b^{t},\theta^{t};\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{i},\tilde{y}_{i}),

\displaystyle\ g_{\theta}^{t}=\frac{1}{B}\sum_{i=1}^{B}\nabla_{\theta}\phi(\mathbf{z}^{t+1},a^{t},b^{t},\theta^{t};\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{i},\tilde{y}_{i})

Finally, update the model and
\bm{\alpha}
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}^{t+1}=\mathbf{w}^{t}-\eta_{w}\mathbf{g}_{w}^{t},\ a^{t+1}=a^{t}-\eta_{a}g_{a}^{t},\ b^{t+1}=b^{t}-\eta_{b}g_{b}^{t},\ {\theta}^{t+1}=\mathcal{P}_{{\Omega}}\left(\theta^{t}+\eta_{\theta}\mathbf{g}_{\theta}^{t}\right)

Table
4
is the dataset description in our experimental setting.
For the benchmark datasets, the training and validation splits are set as follows: 19k/1k for CatvsDog, 45k/5k for CIFAR10, 45k/5k for CIFAR100, and 4k/1k for STL10. The learning rate is initially 0.1, with a reduction by a factor of 10 at
50\%
and
75\%
of total training time. Figure
3
shows the training loss under different imbalanced ratios, which illustrates the convergence of our CODA-Primal algorithm across the four datasets. The testing AUC scores and accuracy results are detailed in Table
5
, where we compare our CODA-Primal algorithm with the PDSCA algorithm in the work
[
41
]
. We also compare CODA with SCGDA
[
9
]
on testing AUC performance, and the results are shown in Figure
4
.

Task-agnostic Robust MAML:

In task robust MAML, we aim to train a model that exhibits consistent performance across all observed task distributions, thereby ensuring robustness against task shifts at test time. The objective can be formulated as the following minimax problem:

\displaystyle\min_{\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\max_{\bm{\alpha}\in\Delta^{N}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha(i)\ell_{i}^{\mathrm{test}}(\mathbf{w}-\eta\nabla\ell_{i}^{\mathrm{train}}(\mathbf{w}))

where
\ell_{i}^{\mathrm{train}}(\mathbf{w}):=\frac{1}{|S_{i}^{\mathrm{train}}|}\sum_{\xi\in S_{i}^{\mathrm{train}}}\ell(\mathbf{w};\xi)
,
\ell_{i}^{\mathrm{test}}(\mathbf{w}):=\frac{1}{|S_{i}^{\mathrm{test}}|}\sum_{\xi\in S_{i}^{\mathrm{test}}}\ell(\mathbf{w};\xi)
. This constitutes a nonconvex-concave compositional minimax problem involving the primal variable. At time t, sample minibatch
\xi^{\mathrm{train}}_{1},...,\xi^{\mathrm{train}}_{N}
from the training set of tasks
1,...,N
, and similarly for
\xi^{\mathrm{test}}_{1},...,\xi^{\mathrm{test}}_{N}
.

First, update the auxiliary variable:

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}^{t+1}_{i}=(1-\beta^{t})\left(\mathbf{z}_{i}^{t}+(\mathbf{w}^{t}-\eta\nabla\ell_{i}(\mathbf{w}^{t};\xi^{\mathrm{train}}_{i}))-(\mathbf{w}^{t-1}-\eta\nabla\ell_{i}(\mathbf{w}^{t-1};\xi^{\mathrm{train}}_{i}))\right)+\beta^{t}(\mathbf{w}^{t}-\eta\nabla\ell_{i}(\mathbf{w}^{t};\xi^{\mathrm{train}}_{i}))\forall i\in[N]

Next, compute the gradients:

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{\alpha}^{t}

\displaystyle=[\ell^{\mathrm{test}}_{1}(\mathbf{z}_{1}^{t+1};\xi^{\mathrm{test}}_{1}),...,\ell^{\mathrm{test}}_{N}(\mathbf{z}_{N}^{t+1};\xi^{\mathrm{test}}_{N})],

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{w}}^{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha^{t}(i)\nabla\ell_{i}^{\mathrm{test}}(\mathbf{z}_{i}^{t+1};\xi^{\mathrm{test}}_{i})({\bf I}-\eta\nabla^{2}\ell_{i}^{\mathrm{train}}(\mathbf{w}^{t};\xi_{i}^{\mathrm{train}}))

Finally, update the model and the mixture weights
\bm{\alpha}
:

\displaystyle{\bm{\alpha}}^{t+1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{{\Delta^{N}}}\left(\bm{\alpha}^{t}+\eta_{\alpha}\mathbf{g}_{\bm{\alpha}}^{t}\right),\mathbf{w}^{t+1}=\mathbf{w}^{t}-\eta_{w}\mathbf{g}_{w}^{t}

It’s notable that
({\bf I}-\eta\nabla^{2}\ell_{i}^{\mathrm{train}}(\mathbf{w}^{t};\xi_{i}^{\mathrm{train}}))
involves the Hessian matrix
\nabla^{2}\ell_{i}^{\mathrm{train}}(\mathbf{w}^{t};\xi_{i}^{\mathrm{train}})
. Inspired by practical implementations of MAML
[
8
]
that simply ignore the second-order term, we use the same trick in our experiments. Our experimental framework involves a range of tasks, each comprising several instances. Contrasting with the standard few-shot image classification including characters from different alphabets, we consider a 5-way 1-shot classification problem involving characters from 5 distinct classes (characters) which are from a specific alphabet. Note that during meta-training, we first sample an alphabet uniformly, then sample an
N
-way,
K
-shot problem uniformly from that alphabet. The convergence of our CODA-Primal algorithm for cases with
K=5
and
K=20
is illustrated in Figure
6
, which shows the meta-training, meta-validation, and meta-testing loss. Figure
5
compares the accuracies in different settings, which shows that the accuracies in 5-way 1-shot learning are higher than those in 20-way 1-shot learning.

Mixture Weights Estimation in Multi-source Domain Adaptation:

Consider
N
source domains
\mathcal{D}_{1},...,\mathcal{D}_{N}
and a single target domain
\mathcal{T}
. The objective is to train a model on a mix of multiple source domains to achieve high accuracy on the target domain. The proposed algorithm consists of two stages.

Phase I: mixing weight estimation

We are aimed at solving:

\displaystyle\min_{\alpha\in\Delta_{N}}\max_{\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{W}}F(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{w})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\alpha(j)g(f_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathbf{w})-f_{j}(\mathbf{w}))+C{\sum\nolimits_{j=1}^{N}\frac{\alpha^{2}(j)}{m_{j}}},

where
g(x)=\sqrt{x^{2}+c}
is the approximator of the absolute value and
f_{\mathcal{T}},f_{1},...,f_{N}
represent loss functions realized by target domain and source domains (training) data. Here,
m_{1},...,m_{N}
denote the number of training data from source
1,...,N
.

Group

Classes

Domains per Group

Samples per Domain

5

1500

Cats, Deer, Dogs

5

1500

Frogs, Horses, Ships, Trucks

5

1500

4

Groups 1 & 2 Combined

5

1500

Table 6
:
Classes and Samples per Domain for Each CIFAR-10 Group

Phase II: Weighted empirical risk minimization

After we obtain the mixture weight
\bm{\alpha}
, we perform empirical risk minimization on the mixed domain:

\displaystyle\hat{h}=\arg\min_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha(i)\mathcal{L}_{\hat{\mathcal{D}}_{i}}(h).

Subsequently, we evaluate
\hat{h}
on the target domain’s test data and present the accuracy findings. The CIFAR-10 non-IID data generation has been shown in Table
6
.

Appendix B
Details of Variance Reduction Algorithm

In this section we present whole algorithm pseduo-code of CODA-Primal+, as in Algorithm
5
. We define the following notations for the convenience of presenting:

\displaystyle G_{k}^{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z},\mathbf{y};\mathcal{I})=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}|}\sum_{(\zeta,\xi)\in\mathcal{I}}\nabla_{1}f(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{y};\zeta)\nabla g(\mathbf{x};\xi)+\mu_{x}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{0}),G_{k}^{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z},\mathbf{y};\mathcal{I})=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}|}\sum_{(\zeta,\xi)\in\mathcal{I}}\nabla_{2}f(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{y};\zeta).

Roughly speaking, we use large mini-batch every
\tau
iterations to update gradients and inner function estimation variable, and use small mini-batch for the rest of iterations.

Algorithm 5

CODA-Primal+

Input:

{\mathbf{x}}^{0},{\mathbf{y}}^{0}
,
\mu_{\mathbf{x}}
, stepsizes
\eta_{\mathbf{x}},\eta_{\mathbf{y}}
.

for

k=0,...,K-1

do

Construct
F_{k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})+\frac{\mu_{\mathbf{x}}}{2}{\left\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}_{k}\right\|^{2}}

(\mathbf{x}_{k+1},\mathbf{y}_{k+1})=\texttt{CODA-SCSC+}(F_{k},\mathbf{x}_{k},\mathbf{y}_{k},T_{k})

end for

Algorithm 6

CODA-SCSC+
(F,\mathbf{x}^{0},\mathbf{y}^{0},T)

Input:

{\mathbf{x}}^{0},{\mathbf{y}}^{0}
,
\mathbf{z}^{0}
such that
{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\mathbf{z}^{0}-g(\mathbf{x}^{0})\right\|^{2}\leq\delta
.

for

t=0,...,T-1

do

if

t\mod\tau=0

then

Sample mini-batch
\mathcal{B}^{t}_{\mathbf{z}}=\left\{\xi_{1},...,\xi_{B_{\tau}}\right\}
, and
\mathcal{B}^{t}_{\mathbf{y}}=\left\{\xi_{1},...,\xi_{B_{\tau}}\right\}

g^{t}=g(\mathbf{x}^{t};\mathcal{B}^{t}_{\mathbf{z}})
,
\mathbf{z}^{t+1}=(1-\beta)(\mathbf{z}^{t}+g(\mathbf{x}^{t};\mathcal{B}^{t}_{\mathbf{z}})-g(\mathbf{x}^{t-1};\mathcal{B}^{t}_{\mathbf{z}}))+\beta g^{t}

\mathbf{q}^{t}=G_{k}^{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{x}^{t},\mathbf{z}^{t+1},\mathbf{y}^{t};\mathcal{B}^{t}_{\mathbf{y}})

end if

else

Sample mini-batch
\mathcal{I}^{t}_{\mathbf{z}}=\left\{\xi_{1},...,\xi_{B}\right\}
, and
\mathcal{I}^{t}_{\mathbf{y}}=\left\{\xi_{1},...,\xi_{B}\right\}

g^{t}=g^{t-1}+g(\mathbf{x}^{t};\mathcal{I}^{t}_{\mathbf{z}})-g(\mathbf{x}^{t-1};\mathcal{I}^{t}_{\mathbf{z}})
,
\mathbf{z}^{t+1}=(1-\beta)(\mathbf{z}^{t}+g(\mathbf{x}^{t};\mathcal{I}^{t}_{\mathbf{z}})-g(\mathbf{x}^{t-1};\mathcal{I}^{t}_{\mathbf{z}}))+\beta g^{t-1}

\mathbf{q}^{t}=\mathbf{q}^{t-1}+G_{k}^{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{x}^{t},\mathbf{z}^{t+1},\mathbf{y}^{t};\mathcal{I}^{t}_{\mathbf{y}})-G_{k}^{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{x}^{t-1},\mathbf{z}^{t},\mathbf{y}^{t-1};\mathcal{I}^{t}_{\mathbf{y}})

end if

\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{y}}^{t}=2\mathbf{q}^{t}-\mathbf{q}^{t-1}

\mathbf{y}^{t+1}=\mathbf{y}^{t}+\eta_{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{y}}^{t}-\nabla r(\mathbf{y}^{t+1}))

if

t\mod\tau=0

then

Sample mini-batch
\mathcal{B}^{t}_{\mathbf{x}}=(\mathcal{B}^{t}_{\mathbf{x},f},\mathcal{B}^{t}_{\mathbf{x},g})=(\left\{\zeta_{1},...,\zeta_{B_{\tau}}\right\},\left\{\xi_{1},...,\xi_{B_{\tau}}\right\})

\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{x}}^{t}=G_{k}^{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{x}^{t},\mathbf{z}^{t+1},\mathbf{y}^{t+1};\mathcal{B}^{t}_{\mathbf{x}})

end if

else

Sample mini-batch
\mathcal{I}^{t}_{\mathbf{x}}=(\mathcal{I}^{t}_{\mathbf{x},f},\mathcal{I}^{t}_{\mathbf{x},g})=(\left\{\zeta_{1},...,\zeta_{B}\right\},\left\{\xi_{1},...,\xi_{B}\right\})

\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{x}}^{t}=\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{x}}^{t-1}+G_{k}^{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{x}^{t},\mathbf{z}^{t+1},\mathbf{y}^{t+1};\mathcal{I}^{t}_{\mathbf{x}})-G_{k}^{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{x}^{t-1},\mathbf{z}^{t},\mathbf{y}^{t};\mathcal{I}^{t}_{\mathbf{x}})

end if

\mathbf{x}^{t+1}=\mathbf{x}^{t}-\eta_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{x}}^{t}+\nabla h(\mathbf{x}^{t}))

end for

Output:

(\hat{\mathbf{x}},\hat{\mathbf{y}})=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}({\mathbf{x}}^{t},{\mathbf{y}}^{t})

Appendix C
Proof of Primal Composition Setting

In this section we provide the proof of results in primal compostion setting (Theorem
1
and Theorem
2
).

The following Lemma is from
[
1
]
, which bound the tracking error of the stochastic correction algorithm.

Lemma 2

(Tracking Error
[
1
]
)
.

For Algorithm
1
, under the assumptions of Theorem
1
, the following statement holds true:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\mathbf{z}^{t+1}-g(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(1-\beta)^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\mathbf{z}^{t}-g(\mathbf{x}^{t-1})\right\|^{2}+4(1-\beta)^{2}G_{g}^{2}\left\|\mathbf{x}^{t}-\mathbf{x}^{t-1}\right\|^{2}+2\beta^{2}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{B}.

C.1
Proof of Nonconvex-strongly-concave Setting

Lemma 3
.

For Algorithm
1
, under the assumptions of Theorem
1
, the following statement holds true:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{t+1})]

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{E}}[\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{t})]-\frac{\eta_{\mathbf{x}}}{2}{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}-\left(\frac{\eta_{\mathbf{x}}}{2}-{\eta_{\mathbf{x}}^{2}\kappa L}\right){\mathbb{E}}\left\|\bar{\mathbf{g}}_{\mathbf{x}}^{t}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+{\eta_{\mathbf{x}}}G_{g}^{2}L^{2}_{f}{\mathbb{E}}\left\|g(\mathbf{x}^{t})-\mathbf{z}^{t+1}\right\|^{2}+{\eta_{\mathbf{x}}}G_{g}^{2}L^{2}_{f}{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t})-\mathbf{y}^{t}\right\|^{2}+\frac{2\eta_{\mathbf{x}}^{2}\kappa L(G_{f}^{2}\sigma^{2}+G_{g}^{2}\sigma^{2})}{B},

where
\bar{\mathbf{g}}_{\mathbf{x}}^{t}=\nabla_{1}f(\mathbf{z}^{t+1},\mathbf{y}^{t})\nabla g(\mathbf{x}^{t})
.

Proof.

According
\kappa L+L
smoothness [Lemma 4.3
[
19
]
] of
\Phi
and updating rule for
\mathbf{x}
, we have:

\displaystyle\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{t+1})

\displaystyle\leq\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{t})+\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{t}),\,\mathbf{x}^{t+1}-\mathbf{x}^{t}\right\rangle+{\kappa L}\left\|\mathbf{x}^{t+1}-\mathbf{x}^{t}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{t})-\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{t}),\,\eta_{\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{x}}^{t}\right\rangle+{\kappa L}\eta_{\mathbf{x}}\left\|\mathbf{g}^{t}_{\mathbf{x}}\right\|^{2},

where
\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{x}}^{t}=\frac{1}{B}\sum_{(\zeta,\xi)\in\mathcal{B}^{t}}\nabla_{1}f(\mathbf{z}^{t+1},\mathbf{y}^{t};\zeta)\nabla g(\mathbf{x}^{t};\xi)
. Notice the fact that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\nabla_{1}f(\mathbf{z}^{t+1},\mathbf{y}^{t};\zeta)\nabla g(\mathbf{x}^{t};\xi)-\nabla_{1}f(\mathbf{z}^{t+1},\mathbf{y}^{t})\nabla g(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}\leq 2G_{f}^{2}\sigma^{2}+2G_{g}^{2}\sigma^{2}.

Taking expectation over the randomness of
\mathcal{B}^{t}
yields:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{t+1})]

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{E}}[\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{t})]-{\mathbb{E}}\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{t}),\,\eta_{\mathbf{x}}\nabla_{1}f(\mathbf{z}^{t+1},\mathbf{y}^{t})\nabla g(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\rangle

\displaystyle+{\eta_{\mathbf{x}}^{2}\kappa L}{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\nabla_{1}f(\mathbf{z}^{t+1},\mathbf{y}^{t})\nabla g(\mathbf{x}^{t})+\nabla h(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}+\frac{2\eta_{\mathbf{x}}^{2}\kappa L(G_{f}^{2}\sigma^{2}+G_{g}^{2}\sigma^{2})}{B}.

Due to the identity
\left\langle\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{b}\right\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\left\|\mathbf{a}\right\|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left\|\mathbf{b}\right\|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\left\|\mathbf{a}-\mathbf{b}\right\|^{2}
, we have:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{t+1})]

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{E}}[\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{t})]-\frac{\eta_{\mathbf{x}}}{2}{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}-\frac{\eta_{\mathbf{x}}}{2}\left\|\bar{\mathbf{g}}_{\mathbf{x}}^{t}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\eta_{\mathbf{x}}}{2}\left\|\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{t})-\nabla_{1}f(\mathbf{z}^{t+1},\mathbf{y}^{t})\nabla g(\mathbf{x}^{t})-\nabla h(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}+{\eta_{\mathbf{x}}^{2}\kappa L}{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\bar{\mathbf{g}}_{\mathbf{x}}^{t}\right\|^{2}+\frac{2\eta_{\mathbf{x}}^{2}\kappa L(G_{f}^{2}\sigma^{2}+G_{g}^{2}\sigma^{2})}{B}

Due to [Lemma 4.3
[
19
]
], we know
\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x})=\nabla F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}))
, we have:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{t+1})]

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{E}}[\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{t})]-\frac{\eta_{\mathbf{x}}}{2}{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}-\frac{\eta_{\mathbf{x}}}{2}\left\|\bar{\mathbf{g}}_{\mathbf{x}}^{t}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\eta_{\mathbf{x}}}{2}\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f(g(\mathbf{x}^{t}),\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t}))-\nabla_{1}f(\mathbf{z}^{t+1},\mathbf{y}^{t})\nabla g(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}+{\eta_{\mathbf{x}}^{2}\kappa L}{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\bar{\mathbf{g}}_{\mathbf{x}}^{t}\right\|^{2}+\frac{2\eta_{\mathbf{x}}^{2}\kappa L(G_{f}^{2}\sigma^{2}+G_{g}^{2}\sigma^{2})}{B}

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{E}}[\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{t})]-\frac{\eta_{\mathbf{x}}}{2}{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}-\frac{\eta_{\mathbf{x}}}{2}\left\|\bar{\mathbf{g}}_{\mathbf{x}}^{t}\right\|^{2}+{\eta_{\mathbf{x}}^{2}\kappa L}{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\bar{\mathbf{g}}_{\mathbf{x}}^{t}\right\|^{2}+\frac{2\eta_{\mathbf{x}}^{2}\kappa L(G_{f}^{2}\sigma^{2}+G_{g}^{2}\sigma^{2})}{B}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\eta_{\mathbf{x}}}{2}\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f(g(\mathbf{x}^{t}),\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t}))-\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f(g(\mathbf{x}^{t}),\mathbf{y}^{t})+\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f(g(\mathbf{x}^{t}),\mathbf{y}^{t})-\nabla_{1}f(\mathbf{z}^{t+1},\mathbf{y}^{t})\nabla g(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{E}}[\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{t})]-\frac{\eta_{\mathbf{x}}}{2}{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}-\frac{\eta_{\mathbf{x}}}{2}\left\|\bar{\mathbf{g}}_{\mathbf{x}}^{t}\right\|^{2}+{\eta_{\mathbf{x}}^{2}\kappa L}{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\bar{\mathbf{g}}_{\mathbf{x}}^{t}\right\|^{2}+\frac{2\eta_{\mathbf{x}}^{2}\kappa L(G_{f}^{2}\sigma^{2}+G_{g}^{2}\sigma^{2})}{B}

\displaystyle\quad+{\eta_{\mathbf{x}}}\left\|\nabla_{1}f(g(\mathbf{x}^{t}),\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t}))\nabla g(\mathbf{x}^{t})-\nabla_{1}f(g(\mathbf{x}^{t}),\mathbf{y}^{t})\nabla g(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+{\eta_{\mathbf{x}}}\left\|\nabla_{1}f(g(\mathbf{x}^{t}),\mathbf{y}^{t})\nabla g(\mathbf{x}^{t})-\nabla_{1}f(\mathbf{z}^{t+1},\mathbf{y}^{t})\nabla g(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{E}}[\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{t})]-\frac{\eta_{\mathbf{x}}}{2}{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}-\left(\frac{\eta_{\mathbf{x}}}{2}-{\eta_{\mathbf{x}}^{2}\kappa L}\right){\mathbb{E}}\left\|\bar{\mathbf{g}}_{\mathbf{x}}^{t}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+{\eta_{\mathbf{x}}}G_{g}^{2}L^{2}_{f}{\mathbb{E}}\left\|g(\mathbf{x}^{t})-\mathbf{z}^{t+1}\right\|^{2}+{\eta_{\mathbf{x}}}G_{g}^{2}L^{2}_{f}{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t})-\mathbf{y}^{t}\right\|^{2}+\frac{2\eta_{\mathbf{x}}^{2}\kappa L(G_{f}^{2}\sigma^{2}+G_{g}^{2}\sigma^{2})}{B}.

∎

Lemma 4
.

For Algorithm
1
, under the assumptions of Theorem
1
, the following statement holds true:

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{y}^{t+1}-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t+1})\right\|^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{1}{16\kappa}\right)\left\|\mathbf{y}^{t}-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}+2\left(\frac{4\eta_{\mathbf{y}}}{\mu}+2\eta_{\mathbf{y}}^{2}\right)L^{2}_{f}\left\|\mathbf{z}^{t+1}-g(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+16\kappa^{3}\left\|\mathbf{x}^{t+1}-\mathbf{x}^{t}\right\|^{2}+\frac{2\eta_{\mathbf{y}}^{2}\sigma^{2}}{B}.

Proof.

We notice the following canonical decomposition due to Young’s inequality:

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{y}^{t+1}-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t+1})\right\|^{2}\leq\left(1+\frac{1}{2(2\kappa-1)}\right)\underbrace{\left\|\mathbf{y}^{t+1}-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}}_{T_{1}}+\left(1+2(2\kappa-1)\right)\underbrace{\left\|\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t})-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t+1})\right\|^{2}}_{T_{2}}.

We bound
T_{1}
first. Define
\bar{\mathbf{g}}_{\mathbf{y}}^{t}=\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f(\mathbf{z}^{t+1},\mathbf{y}^{t})-\nabla r(\mathbf{y}^{t})
. By the updating rule we have:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\mathbf{y}^{t+1}-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left\|\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathbf{y}^{t}+\eta_{\mathbf{y}}\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{y}}^{t})-\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{Y}}\left(\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t})+\eta_{\mathbf{y}}\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{t},\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t}))\right)\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\mathbf{y}^{t}+\eta_{\mathbf{y}}\bar{\mathbf{g}}_{\mathbf{y}}^{t}-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t})-\eta_{\mathbf{y}}\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{t},\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t}))\right\|^{2}+\frac{\eta_{\mathbf{y}}^{2}\sigma^{2}}{B}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left\|\mathbf{y}^{t}-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}+2\eta_{\mathbf{y}}\left\langle\bar{\mathbf{g}}_{\mathbf{y}}^{t}-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{t},\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t})),\,\mathbf{y}^{t}-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\rangle+\frac{\eta_{\mathbf{y}}^{2}\sigma^{2}}{B}

\displaystyle\quad+\eta_{\mathbf{y}}^{2}\left\|\bar{\mathbf{g}}_{\mathbf{y}}^{t}-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{t},\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t}))\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\mathbf{y}^{t}-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}+2\eta_{\mathbf{y}}\left\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{t},\mathbf{y}^{t})-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{t},\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t})),\,\mathbf{y}^{t}-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+2\eta_{\mathbf{y}}\left\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f(\mathbf{z}^{t+1},\mathbf{y}^{t})-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f(g(\mathbf{x}^{t}),\mathbf{y}^{t}),\,\mathbf{y}^{t}-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+2\eta_{\mathbf{y}}^{2}\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{t},\mathbf{y}^{t})-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{t},\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t}))\right\|^{2}+2\eta_{\mathbf{y}}^{2}\left\|\bar{\mathbf{g}}_{\mathbf{y}}^{t}-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{t},\mathbf{y}^{t})\right\|^{2}+\frac{\eta_{\mathbf{y}}^{2}\sigma^{2}}{B}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\mathbf{y}^{t}-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}+(2\eta_{\mathbf{y}}-2\eta^{2}_{\mathbf{y}}L)\left\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{t},\mathbf{y}^{t})-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{t},\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t})),\,\mathbf{y}^{t}-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+2\eta_{\mathbf{y}}\left\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f(\mathbf{z}^{t+1},\mathbf{y}^{t})-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f(g(\mathbf{x}^{t}),\mathbf{y}^{t}),\,\mathbf{y}^{t}-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\rangle+2\eta_{\mathbf{y}}^{2}\left\|\bar{\mathbf{g}}_{\mathbf{y}}^{t}-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{t},\mathbf{y}^{t})\right\|^{2}+\frac{\eta_{\mathbf{y}}^{2}\sigma^{2}}{B}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-\frac{1}{2\kappa}\right)\left\|\mathbf{y}^{t}-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}+2\eta_{\mathbf{y}}\left\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f(\mathbf{z}^{t+1},\mathbf{y}^{t})-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f(g(\mathbf{x}^{t}),\mathbf{y}^{t}),\,\mathbf{y}^{t}-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+2\eta_{\mathbf{y}}^{2}\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f(\mathbf{z}^{t+1},\mathbf{y}^{t})-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f(g(\mathbf{x}^{t}),\mathbf{y}^{t})\right\|^{2}+\frac{\eta_{\mathbf{y}}^{2}\sigma^{2}}{B},

where at first step, we use the fact that
\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t})=\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t})+\eta_{\mathbf{y}}\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{t},\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t})))
for any
\eta_{\mathbf{y}}\geq 0

[
29
, Proposition 7.4]
; at fifth step, we use the classic result for smooth concave function:
\frac{1}{L}\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{t},\mathbf{y}^{t})-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{t},\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t}))\right\|^{2}\leq\left\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{t},\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t}))-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{t},\mathbf{y}^{t}),\,\mathbf{y}^{t}-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\rangle
, and at last step we use the
\mu
strongly concavity of
F(\mathbf{x},\cdot)
, and
\eta_{\mathbf{y}}\leq\frac{1}{2L}
.

For the remaining inner product, we apply Cauchy-schwartz inequality:

\displaystyle\left\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f(\mathbf{z}^{t+1},\mathbf{y}^{t})-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f(g(\mathbf{x}^{t}),\mathbf{y}^{t}),\,\mathbf{y}^{t}-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f(\mathbf{z}^{t+1},\mathbf{y}^{t})-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f(g(\mathbf{x}^{t}),\mathbf{y}^{t})\right\|\left\|\mathbf{y}^{t}-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\mu}\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f(\mathbf{z}^{t+1},\mathbf{y}^{t})-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f(g(\mathbf{x}^{t}),\mathbf{y}^{t})\right\|^{2}+\frac{\mu}{8}\left\|\mathbf{y}^{t}-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2L^{2}_{f}}{\mu}\left\|\mathbf{z}^{t+1}-g(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}+\frac{\mu}{8}\left\|\mathbf{y}^{t}-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}.

Similarly for
\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f(\mathbf{z}^{t+1},\mathbf{y}^{t})-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f(g(\mathbf{x}^{t}),\mathbf{y}^{t})\right\|^{2}
we have:

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f(\mathbf{z}^{t+1},\mathbf{y}^{t})-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f(g(\mathbf{x}^{t}),\mathbf{y}^{t})\right\|^{2}\leq L_{f}^{2}\left\|\mathbf{z}^{t+1}-g(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}

Putting pieces together yields:

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{y}^{t+1}-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-\frac{1}{8\kappa}\right)\left\|\mathbf{y}^{t}-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}+\left(\frac{4\eta_{\mathbf{y}}}{\mu}+2\eta_{\mathbf{y}}^{2}\right)L^{2}_{f}\left\|\mathbf{z}^{t+1}-g(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}+\frac{\eta_{\mathbf{y}}^{2}\sigma^{2}}{B}.

We then turn to bounding
T_{2}
.

\displaystyle T_{2}=\left\|\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t})-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t+1})\right\|^{2}\leq\kappa^{2}\left\|\mathbf{x}^{t+1}-\mathbf{x}^{t}\right\|^{2}

Putting pieces together will conclude the proof:

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{y}^{t+1}-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t+1})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+\frac{1}{2(8\kappa-1)}\right)\left(\left(1-\frac{1}{8\kappa}\right)\left\|\mathbf{y}^{t}-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}+\left(\frac{4\eta_{\mathbf{y}}}{\mu}+2\eta_{\mathbf{y}}^{2}\right)L^{2}_{f}\left\|\mathbf{z}^{t+1}-g(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}+\frac{\eta_{\mathbf{y}}^{2}\sigma^{2}}{B}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\left(1+2(8\kappa-1)\right)\left\|\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t})-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t+1})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-\frac{1}{16\kappa}\right)\left\|\mathbf{y}^{t}-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}+\left(1+\frac{1}{2(8\kappa-1)}\right)\left(\frac{4\eta_{\mathbf{y}}}{\mu}+2\eta_{\mathbf{y}}^{2}\right)L^{2}_{f}\left\|\mathbf{z}^{t+1}-g(\mathbf{x}^{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+16\kappa^{3}\left\|\mathbf{x}^{t+1}-\mathbf{x}^{t}\right\|^{2}+\frac{2\eta_{\mathbf{y}}^{2}\sigma^{2}}{B}
